# The session that built this game

Every prompt and reply, in order. Nobody hand-wrote the TypeScript in
this repository — it was built by agents working from these turns, so
this file is closer to the source than `src/` is.

Regenerated by `node tools/session.mjs`. Tool calls and their output are
not here — they are in `session.jsonl` beside this file, with long
results truncated. Many of the messages below are the build loop waking
itself on a schedule rather than a person typing.

---

### 🧑 Prompt — 2026-08-05 18:00 UTC

I want you to build a Mario Kart-type game involving road cones, planes, helicopters, diggers, trains, trucks, and cars at the level of Mario Kart. It should be utterly perfect, joyful and gorgeous, with every single thing done at Nintendo first-party quality—from the racing feel to the spectacle to anything you could think of.

Break the game into the smallest pieces that can be improved and judged on their own—you decide what the pieces are, not me. Fan out sub-agents and have sub-agents tackle each one individually so that the game is utterly perfect. You should /loop on each piece and have a separate sub-agent with fresh context check the actual rendered result and play the real game—never the builder’s summary—to ensure it looks and feels Nintendo first-party. That separate sub-agent should be a really harsh critic, and if it doesn’t look and feel first-party, it should keep going.

Don't stop until each critic is utterly wowed with the quality when compared with the actual Mario Kart. It should literally compare them side by side blind and say which one is better, and when ours loses, name the single biggest gap and send the builder back in. No fixed number of rounds. Between major waves, spawn one fresh agent to play the whole game and smooth everything into one coherent thing.

Keep a simple live progress page updated as you work so I can watch it evolve. Do this in ThreeJS so it runs in a browser. /loop until it's utterly perfect. Fan out sub-agents and ultracode.

### 🧑 Prompt — 2026-08-05 18:32 UTC

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme: `prefers-color-scheme` carries the OS preference, and the viewer's toggle stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, which must override the media query in both directions. The robust pattern is token-level: define the palette as custom properties on `:root`, redefine only the tokens under `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` — style components through the tokens, never directly inside the media query — then redefine them again under `:root[data-theme="dark"]` and `:root[data-theme="light"]`. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-05 19:07 UTC

TypeScript is in and load-bearing — with this many agents editing in parallel, `src/types.ts` means a change that breaks another module fails `npm run typecheck` instead of silently breaking the game.

**Live board:** https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f3a2bf29-ac1d-4b49-823a-df7e894897d1
**PR:** https://github.com/adam91holt/mario.cone/pull/1 (draft, no CI configured on the repo, nothing to fix)

**Where it stands.** The game is playable end to end: eight racers, drift with three mini-turbo tiers, lap and position tracking, chase camera, HUD, all seven machines on a banked circuit with a crest that pops karts airborne. It's a blockout, not a Nintendo game — that's what the waves are for.

The most valuable thing built isn't game code, it's the review harness. `window.__GAME` exposes a deterministic step API, so `tools/capture.mjs` drives and photographs the *real* game headlessly and reproducibly despite software GL. It immediately earned its keep — three defects I'd never have caught by reading code:

- The road mesh was wound backwards and being discarded wholesale by backface culling. Karts appeared to float over bare ground.
- Landing impulses accumulate on simulation events but only integrate on rendered frames — a run of them drove the camera 350m into the air. That would hit a real player on a stalled tab too.
- Ground snapping rewrote the kart's full position from its spline projection each step, which at speed exactly cancelled forward motion. The kart hit 51 m/s and travelled 11 metres.

I also cut the nine-shot review sheet from timing-out-at-290s to 99s by fast-forwarding the simulation without drawing. That's what makes repeated critic loops affordable rather than theoretical.

**Wave 1 is running now** — kart feel, camera, track, art direction, and the cast. Each has an owner agent and a separate critic that captures frames from the running game, compares them against Mario Kart 8, names the single biggest gap, and sends the builder back. No fixed round count.

Two things you should know:

**The blind comparison is honest but not literal.** I can't put real Mario Kart footage in front of a critic. So the protocol is: each critic writes the specific MK8 reference from memory *first*, before looking at a single frame of ours, then does the labelled-blind A/B against that written reference. It's the strongest honest version of what you asked for, and the "MK8 wins" default is baked in — critics are told ours has to earn it.

**This runs in hours, not minutes.** The box has 4 cores, so the workflow caps at 2 agents at a time, and each critic spawns a browser. Wave 1 alone is up to 30 agent-runs. I've scheduled a check-in so it keeps moving on its own, and the board updates as verdicts land.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-06 02:39 UTC

Wave 1 moved to 15 started / 13 reported, and this hour brought the two biggest changes yet.

**The track was properly redesigned.** Cone Canyon went from a ring with even width and two dead straights to nine named corners authored as map waypoints, with banking derived from turn rate. Two rules hold it together: width follows speed (30m where the pack fans out at the start, 17m at the hairpin apex), and nothing stays straight longer than the run to turn one — the connecting sections carry 250–450m of radius, flat out but still needing to be aimed. It now has boost pads, a gravel shortcut across the hairpin that trades top speed for 25 metres, and buttes placed at the ends of straights so every corner exit has something at its vanishing point.

**The lighting ratio was wrong and is now fixed.** Fill was sitting at parity with key, which is exactly why everything read as flat paper — no light side, no dark side. Fill is now about a fifth of key with exposure lifted to compensate. The sun also got constrained to a narrow mid-morning window, for a concrete reason: above ~35° a kart's shadow hides under its own chassis where the player never sees it, below ~25° it streaks across the racing line. A house quarter-turn on the azimuth throws shadows *across* the road rather than straight down it, away from the chase camera.

One thing to flag: the `claude-code-remote` MCP server disconnected mid-session, which took `send_later` with it. I've replaced the check-in with an hourly cron job — note it's session-only, so it dies if this session ends.

Typecheck clean, committed and pushed (`ebee276`).

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-06 22:15 UTC

**Merged, and the loop is now self-sustaining.**

**PR #1 is merged to main** — engine, review harness, physics, camera, track, art direction, and the seven-machine cast. The branch is fast-forwarded and wave 2 is running.

**The blocker is fixed, and it was a good one.** The karts weren't missing — they were being drawn 200 metres behind themselves. The engine derived the frame-blend factor from the fixed-step accumulator, but the capture harness drives `stepFixed` directly, bypassing that loop. The accumulator was left describing time the loop never owned, and I measured an `alpha` of **−438**. Every interpolation silently became an extrapolation. The simulation was perfect the whole time; only the drawing was wrong, which is why nothing errored and why no amount of reading physics code would have found it.

`alpha` is by definition a 0–1 blend, so it's now clamped at the single point that hands it out, with the accumulator floored too — cause and symptom both closed. Notably the camera agent had already hit this, clamped it locally, and left a comment saying the real fix belonged in the engine. It did. That's the parallel-agent setup working: one module's owner diagnosed a shared bug they weren't allowed to fix, and left the evidence where I'd find it.

**Two corrections to things I'd previously implied:**

I marked the five wave-1 pieces as "passed" on the board and then took it back. Wave 1 **stalled** at 13 of 15 agents — two pieces never returned a closing verdict. Their work is merged and verified, but no critic signed them off, so the board now shows 0/15 passed and they're queued for re-judging. Crediting a pass nobody recorded would make the board worse than no board.

The smoke test was also silently too slow — rendering 720 frames and timing out at 200s. It now steps its run-up like the shots do.

**Wave 2 is running:** items (the entire system is missing — without it this is a time trial), effects (a drift currently looks identical to driving straight), and the HUD.

I've set an hourly cron that commits, verifies, captures, updates the board, **opens and merges a PR each wave**, and launches the next one — through AI, audio, world dressing, race flow, menus, more courses, and performance, plus re-judging wave 1. One caveat worth knowing: that job is session-only, so it dies if this session ends.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-07 18:00 UTC

**Wave 2 landed and is merged** (PR #2). 12 agents, 0 errors, 7.2 hours of work.

**All three pieces failed — and that's the system working.** Every blind A/B went to Mario Kart:

| Piece | Score | Biggest gap |
|---|---|---|
| Items | 7.0 | Incoming-projectile warning sits over the vanishing point for **52% of the race** and never exceeds 0.79 opacity — too constant to mean anything, too faint to read |
| HUD | 6.5 | Every racer sits at place 1 through the countdown, so the first thing after GO! is a gold "1st" then **four false lost-a-place alarms** cascading to 8th |
| Effects | 5.5 | Mini-turbo sparks stretch along *world* velocity instead of velocity *relative to the kart*, so each is a 2–3m bar and a charged drift lays ~40 of them across 7m of track, burying the road and the kart |

What's notable is *how* they found these. They stopped eyeballing and started instrumenting: sampling CSS opacity every four fixed steps across 40 seconds of autopilot, reading alpha peaks off the live particle atlas (the dust cell peaks at 0.466 where every other cell peaks at 1.0 — which is why smoke reads as faint grey doughnuts), and computing spark streak length in metres against the length of the cone. That's why the directives name files and line numbers instead of saying "make it prettier".

The drift capture shows how far it's come though — item slot, minimap with the real circuit, lap pips, position plate, a green shell on the road, coins, boost flame on the kart ahead.

**Round 3 is running** with all three directives carried forward via a new `args.carry` mechanism, so the builders open with what the critics proved rather than rediscovering it. The 12-minute liveness check is armed and will restart the wave if the container suspends again.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-08 21:00 UTC

No — **dead again, for the second time.** Round 3's agents last wrote at 18:38; it's 20:58. Two hours twenty minutes stale, and the date has jumped a day, so the container suspended overnight again.

**The root cause of the repeated stalls was my recovery mechanism, not the agents.** I'd been using in-memory cron jobs to detect death-by-suspension — but those jobs live in the same process that suspension kills. `CronList` now returns "No scheduled jobs": the watchdog died in exactly the event it existed to catch. That was a design error on my part, and it's why you've had to be the one noticing twice.

**Fixed properly this time.** The `claude-code-remote` server is reachable again, and its Routines are stored **server-side** — they survive suspension and actively wake this session. Routine `trig_01EAQ...` is now armed hourly, next firing 22:03 UTC. Its first instruction is to prove liveness *by transcript timestamp*, with the two prior stall durations written into the prompt so it doesn't repeat my mistake.

**State:** round 3's ~40 minutes of work is committed and pushed (`27711fd`) — typecheck clean, but honestly unverified beyond that, since neither builder reached its own smoke run let alone its critic. Round 3 is relaunched (`wf_cf402a38-ac1`) with all three measured directives carried forward.

**One caveat on the Routine:** it was created without stored MCP connectors, so if a fired session lacks the GitHub tools it can commit and push but may not be able to open and merge the PR itself. Past fired check-ins did retain those tools, so I expect it to work — but if you see waves landing without PRs appearing, that's the reason, and I'd merge them by hand on the next ping.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-08 23:09 UTC

Alive and working. Newest agent wrote 3 seconds before I checked; 7 started, 5 reported. Committed and pushed (`65995bf`), typecheck clean.

Three verdicts landed — items 6.5, effects 6.5, HUD 7. All still failing, all still losing the blind A/B. But what they're *catching* has shifted from broken to undesigned:

- **Items** — the roulette settles in 120–160ms against its own documented 1.05s, so the box-to-item beat, which happens ~15 times a race, has no drum-roll and no payoff. You drive through a box and the item is just *there*.
- **Effects** — "coloured light but no rubber." No tyre smoke under the sparks at any tier, so a committed slide reads as an energy effect bolted to a kart rather than tyres letting go.
- **HUD** — every number in the game is set in the browser's fallback system sans with a flat drop shadow, so the most-looked-at elements read as a web dashboard.

One honest note: items went 7.0 → 6.5, which looks like regression but isn't necessarily. Each critic is a fresh agent with its own calibration, so scores aren't strictly comparable run to run — the named gaps are the reliable signal, and those are clearly getting more specific.

The critics have also started writing their own instruments rather than using the standard shot list (`tools/items-probe.mjs`, `tools/items-shots.mjs` both landed this round). Next check-in in an hour.

The drift clip is embedded and playing — 6 seconds of a committed drift charging through the mini-turbo tiers and releasing into a boost, at true speed. A second clip (a lap in progress) is recording now and I'll add it when it lands.

Getting video working took three real obstacles, all worth knowing about:

**The ffmpeg here is crippled on purpose.** It's Playwright's bundled build, cut down to exactly what Playwright's own recorder needs: mjpeg in, VP8 out, nothing else. No PNG decoder, and no `image2` file-sequence demuxer — so the standard `-i frames/f%05d.png` fails twice over. Frames are now captured as JPEG and streamed in through `image2pipe`. I converted the 72 already-recorded PNGs rather than throw away ten minutes of rendering.

**Clips record from a pinned worktree, not the live tree.** The first attempt died on `ReferenceError: veil is not defined` because the fx agent was mid-refactor in `particles.ts`. Recording takes minutes; agents rewrite files continuously. `git worktree add /tmp/mc-stable fd7f6be` gives a checkout of the last verified merge that nothing can mutate underneath the recorder.

**The video is true speed, not real time.** Each frame is one `1/fps` step of simulation plus a screenshot, which took up to 19 seconds per frame under contention. The kart genuinely is doing what you see; it just took twenty minutes to draw six seconds of it.

The page is honest about what it's showing — mid-build, software-rendered, no audio yet, and the three measured faults still outstanding are listed with their numbers.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 04:12 UTC

Confirmed. `args` arrives as a **JSON string**, not an object, so `args.pieces` is `undefined` and the script silently falls back to its default `['items','fx','hud']`. Two consequences: wave 3 has spent 90 minutes redoing wave 2's pieces, and the `carry` directives have never once reached a builder.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 04:13 UTC

**Wave 3 was building the wrong thing, and had been for 90 minutes.** Found it, fixed it, relaunched — the new agents are correctly on **Music & sound** and **CPU racers**.

`args` reaches the workflow script as a JSON *string*, not an object. So `args.pieces` was `undefined` and the script silently fell through to its default `['items','fx','hud']`. Two consequences, neither of which announced itself:

- The wave I launched for AI/audio/world spent 90 minutes on a **fourth** polish round of pieces that had already had three.
- **`carry` has never once reached a builder.** Every round has started from a blank slate. The directives I've been carefully threading forward — the blooper luminance numbers, the 16-gon puff, the roulette timing — were all being dropped on the floor.

Worth being precise about what that means: the critiques *did* keep sharpening round on round, but not because the handoff worked. It's because each critic is a genuinely independent agent that re-derives faults from the running game. The loop was working despite the plumbing, not because of it. That's a good property to have discovered, but I'd been crediting a mechanism that was inert.

The fix parses both shapes and **throws on unparseable args rather than defaulting** — a misrouted wave should fail loudly, not quietly build the wrong thing for an hour and a half.

The polish work from the stopped wave is committed (`60cda14`), not discarded. Wave 3 is now genuinely building the three systems that don't exist: AI, audio and world dressing.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 09:37 UTC

**Wave 3 merged** (PR #4). Three systems that didn't exist now do: the game has **sound**, **CPU drivers that plan corners and use items**, and a **populated world** — crowds, floodlights, marshal posts, grandstands, cranes.

All three failed, and the critiques are the sharpest of the build because each critic measured in the units of its own discipline:

- **AI 5.5** — ran two full 120-second races at 10Hz with all eight karts on the AI brain. The field spends **17.5% of every race in the gravel**, falling off at the same two named corners each lap. And a whole 8-kart race produces **128 blue mini-turbos, 1 orange, 0 purple** — 103 of 166 drifts last exactly 0.7s, released the instant tier 1 lands. The CPUs aren't playing the risk/reward loop the game is built on.
- **Audio 6.5** — rendered the drift chain offline and measured **0.09 dB** of difference in the module's own 1900 Hz scrub band between no slip and full slip, against a 6 dB bar. It even noted slip 0.5 measures *lower* than slip 0, which localises the fault to one gain node never being reached.
- **World 6.5** — parked the kart, advanced 1.4 seconds, diffed the frames: nothing in the bottom 60% changed but sky and exhaust. The animated dressing is real, it's just all 200m away where it's three pixels tall.

Triangles went **281k → 723k** while draw calls **fell 375 → 304**. The instancing is holding.

**Wave 4 is running** — race flow and results, the front-end (title, character select, course select), and three more circuits. Confirmed on the right pieces.

One thing worth flagging: adding those pieces, my generator escaped three closing backticks and broke the script. `node -c` isn't a real flag and silently succeeded, so my first "syntax OK" was worthless — `node --check` caught it. Fixed before launching.

## 2. Key Technical Concepts

- **TypeScript + Vite + Three.js 0.185**, strict tsconfig (`verbatimModuleSyntax`, `noUnusedLocals`, `allowImportingTsExtensions`)
- **Deterministic fixed-timestep simulation**: 120Hz `fixedUpdate(dt)` for all gameplay; `update(dt, alpha)` for visuals only; `alpha` is a 0..1 render blend
- **Seeded RNG (mulberry32)** — `ctx.rng`, never `Math.random()` in sim code
- **Synchronous event bus** — events emitted in `fixedUpdate` must resolve in the same step for determinism
- **`window.__GAME` harness** — `step()` (pure sim, no draw), `render()`, `advance()`, `reset()`, `setInput()`, `setAutopilot()`, `snapshot()`, `stats()`, `seek()`. This is how every critic sees the game.
- **File ownership contract** — `src/types.ts` encodes module boundaries as enforced types so `npm run typecheck` catches cross-module breakage
- **Headless capture under SwiftShader** (no GPU): `--use-gl=angle --use-angle=swiftshader --enable-unsafe-swiftshader`
- **Arc-length parameterised track spline** with uniform XZ grid for O(local) `nearest()` queries
- **`mergeStatic()`** — merging a vehicle's static shell into one mesh per material (~4 draw calls per detailed vehicle)
- **Workflow tool** — `pipeline()` over pieces, builder agent → critic agent, loop until pass; concurrency capped at min(16, cores−2) = **2 on this 4-core box**
- **Durable Routines** (server-side, survive container suspend) vs **CronCreate** (in-memory, dies on suspend)

## 3. Files and Code Sections

**`ARCHITECTURE.md`** — The contract every parallel agent reads. Golden rules, frame model, system interface, execution order table (input 10, track 20, ai 25, physics 30, items 50, race 70, camera 80, vehicles 85, fx/audio 90, hud 100), file ownership table, event bus canonical event list, `window.__GAME` spec, art direction.

**`src/types.ts`** — The enforced contract. Key interfaces: `GameContext`, `GameSystem`, `Racer`, `Track`, `TrackSplineLike`, `VehicleDef`, `VehicleModel`, `RaceState`, `HarnessApi`, `Snapshot`. Added mid-build:
```ts
  /** Input the AI authored for this step. Physics reads it in place of the
   *  player's input. Absent for the human player. */
  aiInput?: InputState;
  /** Rubber-band top-speed multiplier written by the AI system and folded into
   *  top speed by physics. 1 = no adjustment. */
  rubberBand?: number;
```

**`src/core/engine.ts`** — Fixed-timestep loop. **The single most important fix in the build:**
```ts
  /**
   * Visual update plus draw. `alpha` blends between the last two fixed states.
   *
   * It is clamped here because every consumer treats it as a blend factor and
   * feeds it straight to `lerp`. Anything outside 0..1 stops being a blend and
   * becomes an extrapolation: a measured alpha of -438 put each kart two
   * hundred metres behind itself, so the karts vanished from their own chase
   * cameras while the simulation drove on perfectly correctly.
   */
  function renderFrame(frameDt: number = FIXED_DT, alpha = 1): void {
    const blend = alpha > 1 ? 1 : alpha > 0 ? alpha : 0; // NaN lands on 0
    ctx.time.alpha = blend;
    for (let i = 0; i < systems.length; i++) systems[i]!.update?.(frameDt, blend);
```
Plus accumulator floor in `tick()`: `if (accumulator < 0) accumulator = 0;`

**`src/physics/kart.ts`** — Arcade kart model. Critical fix:
```ts
      // Correct ride height *along the surface normal only*. Rebuilding the
      // position from the spline projection would also discard the kart's
      // along-track residual, which at speed cancels forward motion outright.
      racer.pos.addScaledVector(_groundNormal, RIDE_HEIGHT - height);
```
Also `topSpeed()` folds in `racer.rubberBand ?? 1`.

**`src/track/index.ts`** — Winding fix (road was invisible):
```ts
    // Winding matters: `right` is tangent x up, so stepping +j then +i winds
    // clockwise seen from above and the surface faces *down*.
    indices.push(a, a + 1, b, b, a + 1, b + 1);
```
Verges wind side-dependently: `if (side > 0) indices.push(a, a+1, a+2, a+2, a+1, a+3); else indices.push(a, a+2, a+1, a+2, a+3, a+1);`

**`src/vehicles/index.ts`** — `ensureModel()` now repairs model and bookkeeping independently so a missing `visuals` entry can't strand a model.

**`tools/capture.mjs`** — The review harness. Shots fast-forward with `step()` and render only `SETTLE = 0.9` seconds. Smoke uses `step(11)` + `advance(1)`. Nine shots: grid, countdown, racing, drift, boost, pack, overhead, offroad, far.

**`tools/trace.mjs`** — State timeline, `--camera`, `--all`, `--manual`, `--fields`.

**`tools/clip.mjs`** — Deterministic video recorder. Key constraints documented in code:
```js
 * The ffmpeg that ships with Playwright is cut down to exactly what Playwright's
 * own video recording needs: **mjpeg in, VP8 out, and nothing else**. There is no
 * PNG decoder and no image2 file-sequence demuxer... Frames are therefore captured
 * as JPEG and streamed in through image2pipe.
```
Serves `--root /tmp/mc-stable` (pinned worktree) because agents rewrite the live tree mid-recording.

**`tools/wave.workflow.mjs`** — The reusable build/critique workflow. PIECES map now has: items, fx, hud, ai, audio, world, flow, menus, courses. Critical fix:
```js
const input = (() => {
  if (!args) return {};
  if (typeof args !== 'string') return args;
  try { return JSON.parse(args); }
  catch (err) { throw new Error(`workflow args were a string but not valid JSON: ${String(err)}`); }
})();
const ids = input.pieces || ['items', 'fx', 'hud'];
const CARRY = input.carry || {};
```
Critic prompt protocol: STEP 1 write the MK8 reference from memory *before* looking; STEP 2 capture and READ the PNGs; STEP 3 blind A/B; STEP 4 verdict with `score`, `pass`, `blindPick`, `biggestGap`, `directive`, `evidence`.

**`tools/progress.mjs` / `progress.template.html` / `progress.state.json`** — Build board generator, inlines frames as downscaled JPEG data URIs.

**`tools/showcase.mjs`** — Gallery generator, inlines stills + webm clips.

**`src/race/director.ts`** — Just heavily rewritten by the flow agent (wave 4): grid → lights → racing → flag → wrap → results state machine, championship grid ordering, pause as a phase not a widget, finish slow-motion, `createOverlay`, `createRaceBook`, `createCup`.

## 4. Errors and Fixes

1. **Ground snap cancelled forward motion** — kart reached 51 m/s but travelled 11m in 12s. `racer.pos.copy(_groundPoint)...` rebuilt full XZ from spline projection, discarding along-track residual. Fixed to adjust only along the normal.
2. **Road mesh invisible** — road and right verge wound backwards, discarded by backface culling. Karts appeared to float over bare ground. Found by *looking at a screenshot*, not reading code.
3. **Camera launched 350m up** — visual impulses accumulate on sim events but integrate only on rendered frames. Bounded at source (`clamp(dipVel ..., -18, 18)`), dip clamped.
4. **Camera snap used stale yaw** — computed `_desired` before correcting heading, landing beside the kart. Reordered.
5. **alpha = −438** — engine derived blend from an accumulator the harness bypasses. Karts drawn 200m behind themselves; simulation was perfect, only drawing was wrong. Clamped in `renderFrame`.
6. **Lap counting started mid-lap** — added `lapDistance()` relative to `startDistance`, racers start at lap −1.
7. **Capture timeout** — 9-shot sheet exceeded 290s. step-then-settle → 99s.
8. **ffmpeg has no PNG decoder** — Playwright's bundled build is mjpeg-in/VP8-out only. Switched to JPEG frames + `image2pipe`.
9. **`args` arrives as a JSON string** — `args.pieces` was undefined, workflow silently used defaults. **Wave 3 spent 90 minutes on the wrong pieces and `carry` never once reached a builder.** Now parses both shapes and throws on unparseable.
10. **Escaped backticks from Python heredoc** broke wave.workflow.mjs. `node -c` is not a real flag and silently succeeded — `node --check` caught it.

**User-caught error (most important):** I reported wave 2 builders as "running" when they had been dead **11.5 hours**. I inferred liveness from files existing and Chrome process count. The user asked *"is it definitely still running?"* which forced a real check. Lesson now encoded in the Routine: prove liveness by **transcript mtime**, use **`ls -t`** (a plain `ls | tail` is alphabetical and once hid the only live agent behind eleven finished ones), and treat **all agents stopping in the same second** as a container-suspend signature.

**Self-correction:** I amended and force-pushed a commit whose message wrongly said smoke was unverified — it had passed, the output just hadn't flushed when I read it.

## 5. Problem Solving

- Established that **typecheck-clean is a weaker gate than I was treating it as** — a commit passed typecheck but failed to boot (`ReferenceError: veil is not defined` from a mid-refactor file).
- Discovered the container **suspends without warning**, killing in-flight agents and any in-memory scheduler. Replaced `CronCreate` (in-memory, died in exactly the event it existed to detect) with a **durable server-side Routine** (`trig_01EAQCQD9TToG7aSacVrcW2s`, hourly).
- Video recording required a **pinned git worktree** (`/tmp/mc-stable`) because a multi-minute recording spans many agent edits.
- CPU contention: 4 cores cannot serve 3 headless browsers. Never run captures while agents are active; never `pkill` on a pattern matching `capture.mjs` (agents run the same command — I once killed a builder's verification run this way).

The uncommitted work turned out to be hiding a real build break, not just a mid-write snapshot. `results.ts` opens a 130-line CSS template literal, and a prose comment inside it quoted a property name with backticks:

```
/* ... `flex: 1 1 0` with a ceiling means the table ... */
```

The first backtick closes the literal, the second reopens it — so the backtick count stays even and the file reads as balanced, while everything between them gets parsed as TypeScript. One line of CSS commentary surfaced as three unrelated `TS1005` errors, which is exactly why I mistook it for an agent mid-write earlier. The tell was src going quiet for two minutes with the error unchanged.

Fixed with `"` quoting, and added it to the golden rules in `ARCHITECTURE.md` — this codebase is mostly CSS-in-template-literal, so it's a trap every UI agent will walk into rather than a one-off slip.

Typecheck is clean. ### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 13:23 UTC

Wave 4 was killed by a container suspend and has been relaunched as wave 5. This one's worth reporting because two of the findings matter beyond the wave.

**All three pieces failed round 1, each with a measured gap** — the loop working as intended:

- **flow, 6.0** — the rocket start is backwards. Pressing accelerate *on* the green (a 0.1s reaction window) gives 1.38s of boost and 119.3m in two seconds; holding through the final beat, which is Mario Kart's actual rocket window, gives nothing and 41.5m.
- **menus, 6.5** — the menu stage casts no shadows. Ground under the digger's tracks reads luminance 96.9 against 42.4 for the asphalt beside it: a pool of light where there should be a shadow. The same machine on the race grid a quarter-second later is correctly darker than its surroundings.
- **courses, 5.0** — all four circuits render as the same brown desert bypass.

**That third one wasn't a courses problem.** The builder authored four genuinely distinct layouts; none of them can look like anywhere, because `theme.ground` is read in exactly one place — `lighting.ts:176`, as a hemisphere-light colour — and never paints terrain, and all thirteen `theme.props` keys have **zero property reads anywhere in `src/`**. They're prose in a data structure. Saltpan Bypass declares near-white salt and photographs within 12 points of an alpine massif; Jackhammer Quarry cannot contain a quarry. So I added a **`themewire`** piece owning render and world to make a course's declaration mean something, and held courses back behind it. This is exactly the built-but-never-connected failure the coherence pass exists to find, and it turned up on its own first.

**A bug in the carry mechanism, caught before it cost anything.** `evidence` is an array in the verdict schema, but a hand-authored carry arrives however it was typed, and `.join` on a string is a TypeError — which doesn't fail loudly. It throws inside a pipeline stage, drops that piece to `null`, and skips it. My first launch of this wave would have reported itself started and quietly built one piece out of three. Fixed to accept either shape, and I verified the relaunch by grepping the builders' own transcripts: the flow builder's prompt contains its carried directive *and* its measured evidence. That's the first time in this build a carried verdict has been proven to reach a builder rather than assumed to.

**On the suspends:** the decisive liveness test turned out to be the age of the main `claude` process, not file mtimes. Both agents' last write was 13:09:43 — the identical second — and `claude` had been alive 2m36s, younger than the gap. mtime alone would have called it healthy for another seventeen minutes. That's now the first check in the hourly Routine, along with a standing preference for shorter waves so the next suspend costs less.

Wave 5 (themewire, flow, menus) is running, everything is pushed at `9ccc909`, and the board is updated.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 15:18 UTC

Another container suspend at 14:40:50 killed wave 5 mid-flight — but this one cost almost nothing, and that's the useful part.

**Both round-1 builders had already finished and self-verified** (typecheck clean, smoke passing on all four courses) before the suspend. Committed at `076492d`:

- **Theme wiring is real now.** `render/theme.ts` is the single place that decides what a theme key means, and it *throws by name* on anything unrecognised — unknown key, two landscapes at once, a non-boolean switch — called before teardown so a bad course fails loudly instead of half-building. Four ground surfaces with their own colour ramps, strata pitch and verge tints anchored on the declared ground colour, plus four procedural detail maps: canyon dust, quarry crusher-run with haul ruts, a saltpan polygon crack network, alpine schist scree. Not one surface at four brightnesses.

- **The rocket start is fixed and measured, not asserted.** The builder re-probed it rather than trusting the change: held from GO−1.00 to GO−0.10 gives rocketStart and **131.8m in two seconds**; GO−1.99 to GO−1.03 is an ordinary start at 63.7m; a first press at GO buys nothing; more than two beats is a 0.62s burnout at 36.0m. A tapped-then-released throttle doesn't count. `race:burnout` now carries `{racer, held, duration}` and both fx and audio consume it, so the penalty reads as smoke and a bogging engine instead of one red HUD flash.

**Resume across a container restart works.** Rather than relaunching, I resumed the dead run with identical args: both finished builders returned from cache instantly, and only the two agents that actually died — a critic mid-judgement and the menus builder — restarted, with their carried directives intact (verified by grepping their new transcripts for the `Observed:` line). A suspend now costs the in-flight agents, not the wave.

That's now the standing instruction in the Routine: **resume, don't relaunch**, with args byte-for-byte identical or the cache misses. Suspends have tightened to every 35–90 minutes, so waves stay short and lean on resume instead of hoping to survive.

  Workflow({scriptPath:"/home/user/mario.cone/tools/wave.workflow.mjs", resumeFromRunId:"<the dead RUN>", args:<the SAME args object, byte-for-byte>})

Relaunch fresh ONLY if resume errors, and then pass earned verdicts as `carry`.
AFTER ANY LAUNCH OR RESUME, VERIFY BEFORE WALKING AWAY: grep 'YOUR PIECE' AND 'Observed:' out of each new agent transcript. The first confirms the piece, the second confirms carry arrived. A carry bug once dropped two of three pieces to null silently while the wave reported itself started.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 21:10 UTC

Routine tick — wave 5 alive, committed at `8cad3f2`. `claude` up 3h59m, 16 started, 11 returned. Two round-2 verdicts landed, and they rhyme:

- **themewire 6.5 (final)** — not one piece of static world geometry casts a shadow. Barriers, masts, pines, grandstands, containers, silos and boulders all meet the ground at a hard edge while the player's kart three metres away lays a crisp directional shadow.
- **flow 7.5 (final)** — nothing in the game ever celebrates. The finish camera *does* execute a real 2.4s, 60-degree victory orbit — onto a machine doing precisely what it did on lap one. Then the results sheet paints the world out at 97% opacity, so whatever it might have done is invisible anyway.

**Both of these escape their own module.** Missing shadows is now the second failure this wave on the same cause — the menu stage failed on it in round 1 — which makes it a lighting decision nobody owns rather than a themewire bug. And flow can't make the machine react at the finish because the vehicle isn't its to touch, nor is the sheet's opacity.

That's precisely the class of gap the coherence pass exists for: things that fall between the ownership boxes and so never get fixed by any piece critic. Both go into it when the wave lands. Menus is still on round 2; typecheck clean throughout.

What makes that diagnostic rather than just a bug: it failed **three separate pieces** — the menu stage in one round, the world geometry in another, and the contact under every machine. Three different agents, three different modules, same root cause. No module owns lighting, so no piece critic could ever close it, and each one could only report it and move on.

The same shape shows up in the event bus. `race:rocketStart` publishes `quality` and `tier`, and all three listeners destructure only `{ racer }` — so a tier-1 start worth 0.55× boost is indistinguishable from a tier-3 worth 1.0×, and the player has no way to learn the window. `race:finish` publishes `{racer, place, time, podium}` and **nothing in `src/vehicles` listens**, which is why the finish camera performs a real 2.4-second victory orbit around a machine doing exactly what it did on lap one.

These are seams between pieces, not faults inside them — precisely what the parallel structure cannot fix and what the coherence pass exists for. It surveys the whole game front to back, closes the seams with whole-repo ownership, and is judged by a critic scoring the game as one work rather than as parts. It's running alone; no wave will start until it's done.

A few things in the workflow that aren't boilerplate:

- **`PLAYWRIGHT_SKIP_BROWSER_DOWNLOAD` on install.** Playwright is a devDependency the capture harness uses; without this its postinstall drags ~150 MB of browsers into a job that never launches one.
- **`progress.html` copied in by hand, plus `.nojekyll`.** The build board is a self-contained page outside the Vite graph so the build doesn't emit it, and Pages otherwise runs the output through Jekyll, which strips any path starting with an underscore.
- **No typecheck gate.** Type errors don't stop `vite build` anyway, and gating the deploy would take the playable link offline during build waves — exactly when you'd most want to look at it.

No Vite change was needed: `base` is already `'./'`, which is what makes the bundle work from `/mario.cone/` rather than a domain root. The build is 1.46 MB of JS, 440 KB gzipped, with zero asset fetches since everything in the game is procedural — so it should load fast even on a phone.

One thing to know before you play it: the coherence pass is mid-flight and hasn't yet fixed the shadow gap I found earlier, so what deploys right now is a world where nothing casts a shadow. It also added a seventh golden rule to `ARCHITECTURE.md`, written from a bug this build actually paid for: **no backticks inside a CSS template literal, not even in a comment.** Most of the UI here is a several-hundred-line tagged CSS string, and a backtick inside one closes it. Because they're written in prose-commented style, the offender is usually a *pair* of them quoting a property name inside a `/* … */` — the literal reopens on the second one, so the count stays even and the file looks balanced while the text between is parsed as TypeScript. That's what turned a quoted `flex: 1 1 0` into three unrelated syntax errors on one line and cost a green build earlier today. Good that it's now in the contract rather than in my head.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 23:26 UTC

Both fixed and live at **https://adam91holt.github.io/mario.cone/** — deployed from `49e89cb`, run green.

**Steering was genuinely inverted, and the cause is a good story.** Physics integrates `yaw += steer × turnRate` against a heading of `(sin yaw, 0, cos yaw)`, so increasing yaw swings the nose toward `+X` — and since the chase camera looks along that heading, `+X` is screen *left*. Positive steer turns left; the right arrow was mapped to positive.

I fixed the **device mapping, not the simulation**. The AI authors its steer from a signed angle about `+Y` and already agrees with physics — every CPU has always driven correctly — so flipping the integration would have inverted the entire field to fix one human. What let this survive: the comment block in `ai/driver.ts` asserted the opposite convention *on every count*, while the maths beneath it was right. The CPUs drove fine so nothing ever contradicted the prose, and whoever wrote the key mapping followed the comment instead of the code. That comment now says what the code does.

I verified it rather than trusting my own derivation, because the harness structurally *can't* test this — `setInput()` writes the virtual input and short-circuits the exact device path that was broken. `tools/steercheck.mjs` drives real key events and projects displacement onto the camera's right vector: **Right → +18.0 m screen-right, Left → −24.4 m screen-left.**

**For the controls**, there's now a coach in two parts:

- **A card** with the full mapping — keyboard *and* gamepad — on the grid and through the countdown, which is the one stretch of a kart racer that's already dead time so reading it costs you nothing. Also on pause, and **`H`** brings it up anywhere.
- **Cues** that appear only when they're relevant. The one you needed: hold an item and **"E — Throw your item"** appears. Take a corner flat for long enough and it suggests holding Space to drift. Sit in the dirt and it tells you the dirt is slower. Each fires once and never nags again.

The item cue is verified in a real browser, not assumed — the test drove until the player picked up a banana and confirmed the prompt rendered at full opacity, with the card correctly hidden during racing.

`E` throws items (also Ctrl or Z), and **Space is drift** — hold it through a corner to charge a mini-turbo, which is where the speed actually is.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-09 23:28 UTC

The coherence survey had actually **completed** before I stopped it — I've resumed, so it came straight back from cache and only the smoothing agent restarted. It found **14 seams**, and its verdict is the sharpest thing any critic has produced here:

> *"A bag of very good parts, and the parts know it — several files carry comments apologising for the space they cannot reach across."*

Its diagnosis is that there are **two products with a curtain between them**. Everything before the flag is a web front-end: Trebuchet MS, keycap chips, its own renderer with stock ACES and no post, 26 sound cues. Everything after the flag is a console game: every word drawn as geometry, gold plates, the studio's own film grade and bloom — and *total silence from its own interface*. The hand-off is the tell: the launch card and the course card say CONE CANYON SPEEDWAY one second apart in two different typefaces, behind two full-screen curtains running at roughly half each other's tempo, disagreeing about which round of the cup you're in.

The worst five:

1. **Two alphabets**, with a one-second hard cut between them
2. **The front-end has 26 sound cues; the race interface makes none**
3. **Finishing last fires the same confetti cannon as winning** — sixth of eight gets the sad fanfare, the drained frame *and* a full cannon simultaneously
4. **`camera:shot` is emitted for the grid, the countdown and the podium, and nothing has ever listened** — all three shots the race director politely asks for go into an empty room
5. **The weapons are named out of another studio's game** — Bananas and Bob-ombs — on the one nameplate still set in a system font

That fourth one is the dead-end category I expected to bite, and it's worse than I guessed: eleven events are emitted every race with no listener at all, including the wrong-way alarm, which is why *the wrong-way alarm has no alarm*.

Its closing line is the right read: *"None of this is a quality problem; every piece is above the bar its own critic set. It is an authorship problem, and it is fixable mostly by deleting the second copy of things."*

Board updated and pushed (`8924725`), smoothing underway. Your two bugs are already live on the URL — worth another lap whenever you want one.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 00:21 UTC

Alive — `claude` up 7h11m, agent writing 1 second ago. The smoothing agent finished round 1 and built itself a `journey.mjs` tool to walk the whole game.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 00:21 UTC

Routine tick — coherence pass alive, committed at `0d1f3e6`. `claude` up 7h11m, agent writing 1s ago; 4 started, 2 returned. Round-1 smoothing is done and the judge is running.

Worth reporting one thing: **the pass found a bug in the coach card I shipped a few hours ago, and its diagnosis is better than mine.** I gated the card on `race.phase` being `intro` or `countdown`. But the race doesn't stop existing when the menus are up — it's built at boot and keeps simulating behind an opaque front-end, so while you're sitting on the title screen the phase walks `intro → countdown → racing` underneath you. So the card appeared over the title screen and the machine-select roster for the first few seconds and then vanished for no reason a player could see. It's now gated on the front-end's own state instead.

It also cut the card down to **the device you're actually holding**. Both lists at once was thirteen rows, a third of the frame on the grid, sitting over two machines of the 2×4 — and half of it was always wrong for whoever was reading it. `inputState.source` already tracks which device was last touched (it's how the front-end decides whether to say ENTER or Ⓐ), so the card shows that half and swaps the moment you pick up the other thing.

It also built `tools/journey.mjs` — a walk of the whole game front to back, which nothing did before. `capture.mjs` photographs the race; nothing photographed the *seam* between the race and everything around it.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 01:18 UTC

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 01:18 UTC

Container restarted again and killed the run; resumed, and round 2 is underway with the prior directive carried in. Committed at `c1187e0`.

**Round 1 scored the whole game 7.0/10**, still picking Mario Kart in the blind A/B — and its gap is worth far more than the score, because no piece critic could ever have found it:

> *"The front-end is first-party and the race it launches into has no stakes — eight racers finish 6.5 km of Cone Canyon inside **0.811 seconds** of each other, while neither the AI nor the autopilot can hold the tarmac, so the drift→mini-turbo loop the class-select screen calls 'the real game' is something the game never once shows a player happening."*

The measurements behind it: 1st place 3:53.734, 8th at +0.811. A constant steer input oscillates road→dirt→road every ~1.5 seconds and sawtooths speed from 48 down to 32 m/s. And releasing a tier-2 drift **by hand** fires `kart:boost` source `drift2` and takes you 43 → 72.6 m/s — so the loop works perfectly; the AI simply never uses it.

This is the clearest vindication of the whole-game pass so far. The `ai` piece **passed its own critic** — because that critic only ever watched it *drive*, never watched it *race*. It took a judge looking at the finishing spread across eight racers over three laps to see that the rubber-band is strangling the entire competitive layer. I've marked `ai` failed against that measurement.

It also matches what you'd feel on the live build: everyone finishing in a bunch, and never seeing a rival do the thing the game tells you is the point. Round 2 is aimed at the racing rather than the chrome.

**A backtick inside a CSS template literal closes it** — even inside a `/* comment */` quoting a property name. Because these files are written in prose-commented style the offender is usually a *pair*, so the literal reopens on the second one, the count stays even, the file looks balanced, and meanwhile the text between them is parsed as TypeScript. That cost a green build once and surfaced as three unrelated syntax errors on one line.

**`bus.inspect()` is now documented as the dead-event check** — because a listener count of zero is invisible; it looks identical to every other unused entry. That's how eleven events came to be emitted every race into a room with nobody in it, including all three camera shots the race director asks for, and the wrong-way alarm. Which is why the wrong-way alarm has no alarm.

Round 1 measured that same field finishing **inside 0.811 seconds** — the entire eight-racer field, over three laps and 6.5 km, in under a second. **The review sheet had been lying, and that's the finding with the longest reach.** Every piece critic in this project judges from `shots/index.json`. Round 1 proved it was the game's own showcase failing to depict the game:

| shot | was | now |
|---|---|---|
| `drift` | `drifting:false`, tier 0, surface `"air"` | drifting, on road, 47.1 m/s |
| `boost` | kart **not rendered anywhere in frame** | player in frame, 43.2 m/s |
| `pack` | 1.4 m/s, parked against a barrier | 40.6 m/s, 2nd place |

Three of nine marquee frames didn't show what they were captioned, and the player was 8th in seven of nine. Some fraction of every earlier verdict was made looking at those.

**The camera now looks at you.** The chase rig sat so high and far the kart was 4–8% of frame height — 60–70 px of 900 — so the faces, sparks, flames and the win all played too small to land. I checked `drift.png` by eye: the kart fills a proper share of frame now, tier sparks streaming, a rival digger alongside.

**Two rounds scored 7.0 then 6.5.** The drop is the critic reaching deeper, not the game regressing — round 2 measured framing and screen composition that round 1 never got to.

Still open and visible in the frames: the item slot is an empty black square dead centre of the top edge, the results sheet covers the podium it just lit, and the field overcorrected into three clumps rather than a spread.

I've launched the next short wave on **hud + perf** — the item slot because it's in every frame this game will ever be photographed in, and perf because nothing has ever measured whether this holds 60fps off this box. Routing and carry verified in the agents' own transcripts. That's wave one of the three I proposed; your call on the endpoint still stands whenever you want to make it.

Concurrency is 2 on this 4-core box.

STEP 2 — If DEAD, RESUME, DO NOT RELAUNCH. This is proven to work across a container restart:
  Workflow({scriptPath:"/home/user/mario.cone/tools/wave.workflow.mjs", resumeFromRunId:"<the dead RUN>", args:<the SAME args object, byte-for-byte>})
Completed agent() calls return from cache instantly and only the killed agents re-run, carry intact. Verified on 2026-08-09: a suspend killed a critic and a builder mid-flight, resume served both finished builders from cache and restarted only the two that died. Args must match exactly or the cache misses.
First: `npx tsc --noEmit`, then commit and push whatever exists (say in the message if unverified). Read the dead run's journal for results that already have a `score` — those verdicts are earned and must never be re-bought.
Relaunch fresh ONLY if resume errors, and then pass earned verdicts as `carry`.
AFTER ANY LAUNCH OR RESUME, VERIFY BEFORE WALKING AWAY: grep 'YOUR PIECE' AND 'Observed:' out of each new agent transcript. The first confirms the piece, the second confirms carry arrived. A carry bug once dropped two of three pieces to null silently while the wave reported itself started.

STEP 3 — If ALIVE: `npx tsc --noEmit`. If clean, commit and push. If it fails ONLY inside a file an agent is mid-write on, that is transient — commit anything under tools/ that is ready and wait. Do NOT run captures or smoke while agents are active, and never pkill on any pattern matching "capture.mjs".

STEP 4 — If FINISHED:

  b. `node tools/capture.mjs` and LOOK at the PNGs with Read. Never trust an agent's summary.
  c. Update tools/progress.state.json with the real verdicts, `node tools/progress.mjs`, republish the artifact passing url https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f3a2bf29-ac1d-4b49-823a-df7e894897d1
  d. Commit, push, PR (base main, draft false), merge, `git fetch origin main && git merge --ff-only origin/main`. Merge every wave — the user asked for this explicitly.
  e. COURSES is blocked until themewire lands. Once it has, relaunch courses carrying its verdict: it scored 5.0 because theme.ground never painted terrain and all thirteen theme.props keys had zero reads in src, so four distinct layouts all rendered as the same brown desert bypass. themewire has now built render/theme.ts, ground.ts, groundtex.ts, world/themes.ts and landprops.ts to fix exactly that — so courses' job this round is to USE them and prove the four courses are distinguishable from the overhead shot without the minimap.
  f. Then the COHERENCE PASS, which the user asked for and which has still never run: Workflow({scriptPath:"/home/user/mario.cone/tools/coherence.workflow.mjs", args:{rounds:2}}). It must run ALONE — whole-repo ownership, so no build wave in flight.
  g. Then perf: args {pieces:["perf"], rounds:2}.
  h. Then the polish wave carrying the wave 2 directives as `carry`: items (blooper darkens 66% of the central play area at 0.97 opacity for 6s — needs fraction-below-lum-60 under 0.40), fx (puff cell is a 16-gon with straight facet edges — no traceable straight edge at 9x zoom), hud (roulette drum runs 0.067s of a 1.05s spin — must run the whole spin with an ease-out and a landing punch).
  i. Also re-judge wave 1 (feel, camera, track, look, cast) — merged but never given a closing verdict.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 06:38 UTC

Wave 6 alive, both pieces judged and sent back. Committed at `5377314`, typecheck clean.

**`perf` scored 5.0**, and the finding inverts what the piece was for — this is the kind only measurement catches, and it matters directly for you playing it:

> The quality governor sat at its **top rung for 331 seconds at roughly 4 fps** without a single change — on exactly the class of machine it exists to protect.

Two causes, both in how it counts. Its warm-up gate is counted in *rendered frames*, and 240 of those cost 140 seconds on a slow box — so the governor is still "warming up" long after a player would have quit. And the number it reads once it does look **under-reports a GPU-bound frame by twentyfold**, so it sees a healthy frame time on a machine managing four frames a second. A quality ladder that never engages is worse than none, because it advertises protection it doesn't provide.

**`hud` scored 6.5** — the minimap draws all seven rivals as identical flat dots roughly as wide as the road ribbon itself. Any time the pack bunches, which is the whole of lap 1 and every corner after it, they merge into one clump that also covers the chequer. The one readout that exists to say where everyone is says nothing at precisely the moments it matters.

Both are in round 2 now.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 07:54 UTC

**Game:** https://adam91holt.github.io/mario.cone/
**Build board:** https://adam91holt.github.io/mario.cone/progress.html
**Repo:** https://github.com/adam91holt/mario.cone

README is written, merged (`c8cf0f7`) and deployed. It covers:

**How to play** — the full control map, plus the two things that aren't discoverable and are where all the speed is: hold Space *through* a corner to charge a mini-turbo (sparks go blue → green → purple), and hold accelerate through the beat *before* the green for a rocket start, not on it.

**How it's built** — the procedural-everything constraint (no textures, models, audio samples or fonts; every letter on screen is generated geometry), the deterministic 120 Hz simulation, and then the agent process: pieces owned by one agent each with ownership enforced through the type system, and a separate fresh-context critic that writes its Mario Kart 8 reference **from memory before looking at this game at all**, so it can't unconsciously grade on a curve.

I quoted three real verdicts rather than describing the process abstractly, because the specificity is the whole point — ground luminance 96.9 against 42.4 under a digger, a rocket window measured in metres travelled, a quality governor asleep for 331 seconds at 4 fps. Plus the coherence pass and why file ownership makes it necessary, the `window.__GAME` harness, and the tool table including why `steercheck.mjs` had to exist.

**Five screenshots**, 656 KB total. The raw review frames are 1–1.7 MB each, which is right for a critic reading pixel luminance and wrong for a repo that would carry ten megabytes of regenerated screenshot in every clone forever — so a new `tools/readmeshots.mjs` downscales them using the same Chromium canvas trick the build board already uses. No new dependency.

I checked every factual claim against the repo rather than writing from memory: course and vehicle counts, npm scripts, the harness method names, the shot count, and the board tally. And it says plainly that no piece has passed the 8.5 bar yet and scores sit around 6.5–7.5 — a README claiming otherwise would be contradicted by the build board it links to two lines earlier.

Wave 6 (hud + perf) is still running in the background through all of this.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 08:40 UTC

Wave 6 merged (`b8f6115`) and deploying. Two results worth your attention:

**The frame cost came down a quarter without the simulation moving at all.**

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| triangles | 678,804 | **509,822** |
| draw calls | 370 | **299** |
| player speed | 47.825 m/s | 47.825 m/s |
| progress | 512.926 m | 512.926 m |

Speed and progress identical to three decimals on the same seed. That was the perf brief's one hard constraint — LOD and culling may never touch anything the simulation reads — and it held under measurement rather than assertion.

**The item slot is no longer a black hole.** That empty black square dead centre of the top edge, in 18 of 19 race frames, now has a designed hazard-stripe idle and draws what you're carrying. The roulette runs its whole spin instead of 6% of it, and the minimap gives each rival a readable marker instead of seven identical dots that merged into one clump for the whole of lap 1.

**Both still failed, and `perf`'s verdict is the sharpest thing the loop has produced.** Round 1 failed the quality governor because its warm-up gate was counted in *rendered frames*, so it could never fire on a slow machine. The gate is now counted in seconds — and the seconds accumulator has **exactly the same property one layer down**: it discards the very frames the governor exists to measure, so it accrued 1.66 s of "live" time across 200 s of wall clock and sat reading `priming, samples: 0` for three minutes. The fix moved the bug rather than removing it, and the critic caught it in precisely those terms.

`hud` (7.0) is caught against its own reasoning: it deliberately keeps the position plate on screen at the finish because "it is the answer", and the plate comes to rest with its entire ordinal suffix behind the letterbox bar. Win, and you're shown a sawn-off gold `1` with no `ST`.

I've launched the polish wave — **items + fx** — carrying the two measured directives from wave 2: the blooper that darkens 66% of the play area at 0.97 opacity for six seconds, and the dust puff that's a 16-gon with traceable straight edges. Routing and carry verified in the agents' transcripts. That's wave two of the three I proposed.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 12:36 UTC

Polish wave finished — items **5.5**, fx **6.0**, both failing with the most specific findings yet. Running the gates.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 12:40 UTC

The boost frame is dramatic — and the critic's complaint is plainly visible: that **flat pale-gold torus lying in the road plane** under the kart, with the machine's rear blown to white. It does read as a decal rather than light. Landing the wave.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 12:42 UTC

Polish wave merged (`ea0a33f`) and deploying. Both pieces went **down** — items 6.5 → 5.5, fx 7.0 → 6.0 — and in both cases because the critic reached past the gap it was sent to close.

**The items finding is the most important thing to come out of this project so far**, and it's a gameplay problem you'd feel immediately. The critic ran a *full-race census* instead of taking a screenshot — 3 laps, 145 seconds, the game's own CPU driver at the wheel:

- item slot occupied **10.3%** of race time
- **3 draws** in the entire race
- one unbroken **63.7-second** stretch holding nothing — all of laps 2 and 3
- a star, bullet, lightning, red shell or mushroom **never once** reached the player's hand

So the whole item layer, which is genuinely well built, is something a player barely touches. The cause is named exactly: a 4-second box respawn against an 8-kart field on rows of 5 (anyone outside the first five through a row gets bare tarmac), rows spanning only 66% of the drivable width so a wide entry line passes a full row cleanly, and four rows per 2.2 km lap.

Round 1 *did* fix what it was asked to — the blooper no longer blacks the screen out, and the roster is renamed. But round 1 renamed it and left Nintendo's **art**: the slot was still drawing a banana with a stalk and a Koopa shell with three studs. The round-2 critic caught that half-fix by going to the surface the player looks at most.

**fx** killed the 16-gon puff and the whole particle family with it, then got caught on the mini-turbo payoff. I confirmed it by eye on the boost frame: a hard-edged pale-gold torus lying flat *in the road plane* under a machine whose rear half is blown to white, with no flame at any frame and the tier colour thrown away — so a violet ultra and a blue tier-1 pay off identically.

Both are now in a third round with those measurements as their directives, including an explicit acceptance test for items: slot occupied ≥60% of race time, ≥8 draws, never empty longer than ~12 seconds.

### 🧑 Prompt — 2026-08-10 12:51 UTC

MARIO.CONE build loop — hourly. Repo adam91holt/mario.cone, branch claude/mario-kart-racing-game-z8wdf1. WFDIR=/root/.claude/projects/-home-user-mario-cone/e9fc5037-5a81-535b-8c48-d7c7034e82f9/subagents/workflows

Do NOT trust any run id written here — resolve it: RUN=$(ls -t $WFDIR | head -1). Current wave: wave 5 (themewire, flow, menus) = wf_966b0f91-b70.

STEP 1 — PROVE the wave is alive. Two checks; the second has never been wrong:
  date -u
  ls -lt --time-style=+%H:%M:%S $WFDIR/$RUN/agent-*.jsonl | head -3
  ps -eo etime,comm | grep -w claude
(a) Newest agent-*.jsonl mtime older than ~25 minutes = dead. USE `ls -t`; a plain `ls | tail` sorts ALPHABETICALLY and once hid the only live agent behind eleven finished ones.
(b) ELAPSED of the main `claude` process. DECISIVE. If claude has been alive for LESS time than the gap since the last agent write, the container restarted and every in-flight agent is dead, however recent the mtimes look. Two agents whose last write is the IDENTICAL second is the same signature. Do NOT grep for claude in a `--sort=-pcpu | head -4` list — Chrome outranks it and it will not appear.
Never judge by files existing or Chrome counts: subagents run IN-PROCESS inside the main claude process, so `ps aux | grep claude` shows nothing for a healthy wave. Both mistakes have cost silent stalls of 11.5 and 2.5 hours.

Count: node -e "const fs=require('fs');let s=0,r=0;for(const l of fs.readFileSync(process.argv[1],'utf8').trim().split('\n')){try{const e=JSON.parse(l);if(e.type==='started')s++;if(e.type==='result')r++;}catch{}}console.log(s,r)" $WFDIR/$RUN/journal.jsonl
Concurrency is 2 on this 4-core box.

STEP 2 — If DEAD, RESUME, DO NOT RELAUNCH. This is proven to work across a container restart:
  Workflow({scriptPath:"/home/user/mario.cone/tools/wave.workflow.mjs", resumeFromRunId:"<the dead RUN>", args:<the SAME args object, byte-for-byte>})
Completed agent() calls return from cache instantly and only the killed agents re-run, carry intact. Verified on 2026-08-09: a suspend killed a critic and a builder mid-flight, resume served both finished builders from cache and restarted only the two that died. Args must match exactly or the cache misses.
First: `npx tsc --noEmit`, then commit and push whatever exists (say in the message if unverified). Read the dead run's journal for results that already have a `score` — those verdicts are earned and must never be re-bought.
Relaunch fresh ONLY if resume errors, and then pass earned verdicts as `carry`.
AFTER ANY LAUNCH OR RESUME, VERIFY BEFORE WALKING AWAY: grep 'YOUR PIECE' AND 'Observed:' out of each new agent transcript. The first confirms the piece, the second confirms carry arrived. A carry bug once dropped two of three pieces to null silently while the wave reported itself started.

STEP 3 — If ALIVE: `npx tsc --noEmit`. If clean, commit and push. If it fails ONLY inside a file an agent is mid-write on, that is transient — commit anything under tools/ that is ready and wait. Do NOT run captures or smoke while agents are active, and never pkill on any pattern matching "capture.mjs".

STEP 4 — If FINISHED:
  a. `npx tsc --noEmit` then `node tools/capture.mjs --smoke`; fix anything broken. Typecheck-clean has passed on a build that did not boot. Smoke is the real gate.
  b. `node tools/capture.mjs` and LOOK at the PNGs with Read. Never trust an agent's summary.
  c. Update tools/progress.state.json with the real verdicts, `node tools/progress.mjs`, republish the artifact passing url https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f3a2bf29-ac1d-4b49-823a-df7e894897d1
  d. Commit, push, PR (base main, draft false), merge, `git fetch origin main && git merge --ff-only origin/main`. Merge every wave — the user asked for this explicitly.
  e. COURSES is blocked until themewire lands. Once it has, relaunch courses carrying its verdict: it scored 5.0 because theme.ground never painted terrain and all thirteen theme.props keys had zero reads in src, so four distinct layouts all rendered as the same brown desert bypass. themewire has now built render/theme.ts, ground.ts, groundtex.ts, world/themes.ts and landprops.ts to fix exactly that — so courses' job this round is to USE them and prove the four courses are distinguishable from the overhead shot without the minimap.
  f. Then the COHERENCE PASS, which the user asked for and which has still never run: Workflow({scriptPath:"/home/user/mario.cone/tools/coherence.workflow.mjs", args:{rounds:2}}). It must run ALONE — whole-repo ownership, so no build wave in flight.
  g. Then perf: args {pieces:["perf"], rounds:2}.
  h. Then the polish wave carrying the wave 2 directives as `carry`: items (blooper darkens 66% of the central play area at 0.97 opacity for 6s — needs fraction-below-lum-60 under 0.40), fx (puff cell is a 16-gon with straight facet edges — no traceable straight edge at 9x zoom), hud (roulette drum runs 0.067s of a 1.05s spin — must run the whole spin with an ease-out and a landing punch).
  i. Also re-judge wave 1 (feel, camera, track, look, cast) — merged but never given a closing verdict.

Container suspends are now hitting every 35-90 minutes. Keep waves SHORT (two pieces, two rounds) and rely on resume rather than on a wave surviving.

Stop when every piece in tools/progress.state.json is "pass". Message the user only when a wave lands, a wave had to be restarted, something needs a decision, or the build is finished.

### 🤖 Claude — 2026-08-10 16:17 UTC

Round 3 merged (`79d1495`) and deploying. Three things worth reporting.

**The item economy fix worked, and the mechanism is the interesting part.** Last round's critic *measured* the slot as occupied 10.3% of a race. That measurement became this round's acceptance test — ≥60% occupied, ≥8 draws, never empty more than 12 seconds — so the builder had to race three full laps to know whether it had passed rather than eyeball a frame. It passed, and peaked at **7.5**, the largest single-round move this project has recorded.

**But the closing critic found something odd enough to cost it the score.** A single carried item **isn't drawn in the world at all** — the orbit rig is only ever built for multiples, so `carry()` returns nothing for one item. The shield that fires six times a race is something you're protected by and can never see, aim, or plan around. And hold-to-shield doesn't exist: holding the button throws.

**The frame budget went backwards, and that's honest to record:** 475 draw calls and 667k triangles against 314 and 520k, with race pace down 47.7 → 38.4 m/s. The economy fix put 102 boxes in 11 rows on the course. More boxes → more items → more interference; both the draw cost and the slower race are that working as intended, but `perf` now has to be re-run against it.

**And I've stopped treating shadows as a per-piece defect.** Five separate critics have now failed five different pieces on the same cause — the menu stage, the world geometry, the machines, the floor quality rung, the items — each diagnosing it correctly inside its own module, none of them able to fix it. Nothing in the ownership table owns lighting, so it has been reported five times and closed zero. I confirmed it by eye again: player, rivals, barriers and coins all meet the ground at a hard edge.

So rather than a seventh piece wave, I've launched a **coherence pass with whole-repo ownership** carrying all five measurements as its opening directive, plus two things no single piece can own: the purple tier-3 mini-turbo that is built and has **never once been seen** (the player never reached it in 90 seconds of CPU driving), and instancing those 102 new boxes. Verified it started with the directive intact.
